    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi - lo + 1))
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_range_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo), abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)))

            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo), abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi - lo + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the

            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi - sub_lo + 1))

    def test_decoder_accuracy(metrics, submitted_data_full, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Run the provided train_decoder.py script on the sample data and check that it runs without error."""
    
        train_params = {
            'npcs': 100,
            'lr': 1e-3,
            'l1_weight': 1e-4,
            'balanced_loss': True,
        }
    
        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = None
    
        if reference_data_stats is not None:
            ref_accuracy = reference_data_stats['validation_balanced_accuracy']
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_reference'] = ref_accuracy
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = None
    
        # train the decoder on the submitted data
        rng_state = reference_data_stats.get("rng_state") if reference_data_stats else None
        try:
            scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \
                train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],
                                    submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)
        except torch.cuda.OutOfMemoryError:
            print("CUDA out of memory error during decoder training. Retrying on CPU...")
            train_params['device'] = torch.device('cpu')
            scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \
                train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],
                                    submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)
    
        # evaluate accuracy on test set
        test_predictions_only = get_trial_indices(predictions, test_idx)
        test_output_only = get_trial_indices(output, test_idx)
        balanced_accuracy = accuracy_all_sessions(test_predictions_only, test_output_only, balanced=True)
    
        sub_accuracy = {
            submitted_data_full['output_names'][i]: float(balanced_accuracy[i])
            for i in range(len(balanced_accuracy))
        }
        # Persist the raw per-output accuracy as soon as it's computed. Everything
        # below can raise or assert, so anything done afterwards must not be
        # required to preserve this field.
        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = sub_accuracy
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison, skipping accuracy ratio and threshold checks")
            return
    
        # The matching + ratio + threshold checks below are wrapped so that a
        # failure there doesn't prevent the raw accuracy above from being reported.
        # Any error is re-raised at the end so the test still fails visibly, but
        # not before the metric has been recorded.
        deferred_error = None
        try:
            # find a match between output dimensions (same as test_data_stats)
            output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
                submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
            )
    
            metrics['output_match_cost'] = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
    
            # Best-effort ratio: skip any match whose reference or submitted name
            # isn't resolvable (e.g. matcher returned inf cost / None).
            ratio = {}
            for ref_name, sub_name, cost in output_matches:
                if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:
                    continue
                if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy or not ref_accuracy[ref_name]:
                    continue
                ratio[ref_name] = sub_accuracy[sub_name] / ref_accuracy[ref_name]
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = ratio
    
            for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
                if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:
                    continue
                if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy:
                    continue
                ref_acc = ref_accuracy[ref_name]
                sub_acc = sub_accuracy[sub_name]
                assert sub_acc >= MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC * ref_acc, f'Accuracy for output {sub_name} ({sub_acc:.3f}) is below {MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC*100:.1f}% of reference accuracy ({ref_acc:.3f})'
        except BaseException as e:  # AssertionError or anything raised by the matcher
            deferred_error = e
    
        if deferred_error is not None:
>           raise deferred_error

list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. How are the data split into sessions?\n    35→\n    36→i. Each session corresponds to a unique `ophys_session_id` in the experiment table. Trials are defined using the built-in `dataset.trials` table from the Allen SDK. Each trial corresponds to one stimulus change event (go or catch). The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). Aborted trials, auto-rewarded trials, and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded.\n    54→\n    55→ii.\n    56→```python\n    57→trials_table = ref_ds.trials\n    58→...\n    59→valid_trials = trials_table[\n    60→    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    61→    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    62→    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    63→]\n    64→\n    65→for _, row in valid_trials.iterrows():\n    66→    start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n    67→    end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n    68→    if end_idx > T:\n    69→        end_idx = T\n    70→    if end_idx <= start_idx:\n    71→        continue\n    72→    idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n    73→```\n    74→\n    75→iii. The SDK's built-in trials table was used, since it provides pre-computed trial metadata (outcome, image identity, timing). The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n    76→\n    77→## 1-e. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n    80→\n    81→ii.\n    82→```python\n    83→valid_trials = trials_table[\n    84→    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    85→    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    86→    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    87→]\n    88→...\n    89→if end_idx > T:\n    90→    end_idx = T\n    91→if end_idx <= start_idx:\n    92→    continue\n    93→...\n    94→if len(trials) < 2:\n    95→    continue\n    96→```\n    97→\n    98→iii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n    99→\n   100→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n   101→\n   102→i. Neural data is derived from the `dff_traces` (dF/F calcium fluorescence traces) from each experiment in the session, accessed via `dataset.dff_traces.dff`.\n   103→\n   104→ii.\n   105→```python\n   106→for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n   107→    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n   108→neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n   109→```\n   110→\n   111→iii. dF/F is the standard measure of neural activity for two-photon calcium imaging. The Allen SDK provides it pre-computed with neuropil correction and baseline normalization already applied.\n   112→\n   113→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n   114→\n   115→i. The only processing is combining neurons from multiple imaging planes within a session by vertically stacking their dF/F arrays. Each neuron is tagged with a plane label (`{area}_{depth}um`) for brain region tracking.\n   116→\n   117→ii.\n   118→```python\n   119→dff_list = []\n   120→plane_labels = []\n   121→for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n   122→    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n   123→    area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n   124→    depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n   125→    plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\n   126→neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n   127→```\n   128→\n   129→iii. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. Plane labels were constructed to preserve brain region information for downstream analysis.\n   130→\n   131→## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n   132→\n   133→i. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n   134→\n   135→ii. N/A\n   136→\n   137→iii. The Allen SDK pipeline already applies its own quality control (e.g., cell segmentation, neuropil correction). No further filtering was deemed necessary.\n   138→\n   139→## 2-d. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   140→\n   141→i. The neural data is not binned or resampled. It is kept at the native ophys frame rate (~11 Hz). The time bin size is computed from the median inter-frame interval of `ophys_timestamps`.\n   142→\n   143→ii.\n   144→```python\n   145→first_ophys_ts = session_results[0][1]['ophys_ts']\n   146→time_bin_size_ms = float(np.median(np.diff(first_ophys_ts)) * 1000)\n   147→```\n   148→\n   149→iii. The ophys timestamps are already at a consistent frame rate determined by the microscope scanning. No resampling is needed since all data (neural, running, pupil) are aligned to the same ophys timebase.\n   150→\n   151→## 2-e. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   152→\n   153→i. Each trial's neural data is aligned to the trial start (`start_time`). The ophys frames from `start_time` to `stop_time` are extracted, giving a variable-length window per trial.\n   154→\n   155→ii.\n   156→```python\n   157→start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n   158→end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n   159→idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n   160→...\n   161→'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n   162→```\n   163→\n   164→iii. `np.searchsorted` finds the first ophys frame at or after each boundary time. Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n   165→\n   166→## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n   167→\n   168→i. Image name is a time-varying variable derived from both `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` columns in the trials table, combined with `change_time` to determine when the image switches.\n   169→\n   170→ii.\n   171→```python\n   172→change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n   173→image_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\n   174→image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n   175→image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n   176→```\n   177→\n   178→iii. Since the trial window now spans both pre- and post-change periods, image identity varies within a trial. Before `change_time`, the initial image is on screen; after, the change image is displayed. For catch trials (sham change), `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` are the same, so the image identity is constant throughout.\n   179→\n   180→## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n   181→\n   182→i. Image names are mapped to integer codes via a global mapping built from all unique image names across all sessions. The integer code varies within a trial (initial image code before change, change image code after).\n   183→\n   184→ii.\n   185→```python\n   186→all_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\n   187→image_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n   188→...\n   189→image_row = np.array(\n   190→    [image_to_code[name] for name in t['image_names']],\n   191→    dtype=np.int8)\n   192→```\n   193→\n   194→iii. A global mapping ensures consistent integer codes across sessions. Sorting the image names makes the mapping deterministic. The mapping is stored in `metadata['image_to_code']` so it can be recovered for interpretation.\n   195→\n   196→## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n   197→\n   198→i. Image name is computed per ophys frame within the trial window, using the same `idx` array as the neural data. The switch point is determined by `np.searchsorted` on `change_time`.\n   199→\n   200→ii.\n   201→```python\n   202→change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n   203→image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n   204→image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n   205→```\n   206→\n   207→iii. The image identity at each frame is determined by whether that frame falls before or after `change_time`. This is aligned to the neural data because both use the same ophys frame indices.\n   208→\n   209→## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n   210→\n   211→i. Image change is a binary time-varying variable derived from `change_time` and the `go` column in the trials table. It is 1 only during the first image flash and following grey period (750ms window starting at `change_time`), and only for **go trials** where an actual image change occurs. For catch trials (sham change), `image_change` is 0 throughout.\n   212→\n   213→ii.\n   214→```python\n   215→image_change = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=np.int8)\n   216→if row['go']:\n   217→    change_end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'] + 0.75)\n   218→    image_change[change_idx:change_end_idx] = 1\n   219→```\n   220→\n   221→iii. The 750ms window corresponds to one stimulus flash (250ms) plus the following grey inter-stimulus interval (500ms). This marks only the transient change event rather than the entire post-change period. Catch trials are excluded because no actual image change occurs — the `go` column distinguishes real changes from sham changes.\n   222→\n   223→## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image change*?\n   224→\n   225→i. No processing beyond computing the binary indicator from `change_time` and `go` via `np.searchsorted`.\n   226→\n   227→ii. See 3-a.\n   228→\n   229→iii. N/A\n   230→\n   231→## 4-c. How is `output` *Image change* aligned with the neural data?\n   232→\n   233→i. Same as image name — computed per ophys frame using the same `idx` array and `change_time` alignment.\n   234→\n   235→ii. See 3-a.\n   236→\n   237→iii. Same frame-level alignment as image name and neural data.\n   238→\n   239→## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n   240→\n   241→i. Running speed is derived from `dataset.running_speed`, which provides speed and timestamps from the running wheel encoder.\n   242→\n   243→ii.\n   244→```python\n   245→run = ref_ds.running_speed\n   246→```\n   247→\n   248→iii. The `running_speed` attribute is the SDK's standard interface for locomotion data.\n   249→\n   250→## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n   251→\n   252→i. Running speed is linearly interpolated from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values (from extrapolation) are mapped to bin 0.\n   253→\n   254→ii.\n   255→```python\n   256→f_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n   257→                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   258→running_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n   259→...\n   260→all_running = np.concatenate([\n   261→    np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n   262→    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n   263→])\n   264→run_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n   265→...\n   266→run_disc = apply_discretize(t['running'], run_edges)\n   267→```\n   268→\n   269→iii. Linear interpolation preserves the signal shape while resampling to the ophys timebase. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts across bins, which is important for balanced decoding. Bin edges are computed globally across all sessions to maintain consistent categories.\n   270→\n   271→## 5-c. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n   272→\n   273→i. Running speed is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data. The same `idx` array is used to extract both.\n   274→\n   275→ii.\n   276→```python\n   277→run = ref_ds.running_speed\n   278→f_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n   279→                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   280→running_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n   281→...\n   282→'running': running_speed[idx].astype(np.float32),\n   283→'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n   284→```\n   285→\n   286→iii. By interpolating running speed onto `ophys_ts` upfront, alignment is guaranteed — both neural and running data are indexed by the same ophys frame indices. Based on the AllenSDK code, the clock used for different data stream are synced at the hardware level, so we can safely interpolate.\n   287→\n   288→## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Pupil diameter* derived from?\n   289→\n   290→i. Pupil diameter is derived from `dataset.eye_tracking`, using the `pupil_width` column. Blink frames (where `likely_blink` is True) are excluded before interpolation.\n   291→\n   292→ii.\n   293→```python\n   294→eye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\n   295→eye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\n   296→```\n   297→\n   298→iii. `pupil_width` was used as the measure of pupil diameter. Blink frames were removed prior to interpolation to avoid corrupting the signal with blink artifacts. The SDK's `likely_blink` flag provides a pre-computed blink detector.\n   299→\n   300→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n   301→\n   302→i. Pupil diameter is linearly interpolated (after blink removal) from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values are mapped to bin 0.\n   303→\n   304→ii.\n   305→```python\n   306→f_pupil = interp1d(eye_clean['timestamps'].values,\n   307→                   eye_clean['pupil_width'].values,\n   308→                   kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   309→pupil_diameter = f_pupil(ophys_ts)\n   310→...\n   311→all_pupil = np.concatenate([\n   312→    np.concatenate([t['pupil'] for t in trials])\n   313→    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n   314→])\n   315→pupil_edges = discretize(all_pupil, N_LEVELS)\n   316→...\n   317→pup_disc = apply_discretize(t['pupil'], pupil_edges)\n   318→```\n   319→\n   320→iii. Same approach as running speed: linear interpolation to the ophys timebase, then global percentile-based discretization. Blink removal before interpolation prevents blink artifacts from propagating into neighboring timepoints.\n   321→\n   322→## 6-c. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* aligned with the neural data?\n   323→\n   324→i. Same approach as running speed — pupil diameter is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data.\n   325→\n   326→ii.\n   327→```python\n   328→eye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\n   329→eye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\n   330→f_pupil = interp1d(eye_clean['timestamps'].values,\n   331→                    eye_clean['pupil_width'].values,\n   332→                    kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   333→pupil_diameter = f_pupil(ophys_ts)\n   334→...\n   335→'pupil': pupil_diameter[idx].astype(np.float32),\n   336→'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n   337→```\n   338→\n   339→iii. Same as running speed — the eye tracking timestamps are hardware-synced with the ophys clock, so interpolation is valid. Using the same `idx` array guarantees alignment.\n   340→\n   341→## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n   342→\n   343→i. Trial outcome is derived from the boolean columns `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, and `correct_reject` in the trials table.\n   344→\n   345→ii.\n   346→```python\n   347→TRIAL_OUTCOMES = ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']\n   348→...\n   349→outcome = 'other'\n   350→for label in TRIAL_OUTCOMES:\n   351→    if row[label]:\n   352→        outcome = label\n   353→        break\n   354→```\n   355→\n   356→iii. These four columns are the SDK's canonical trial outcome labels for the change detection task. They are mutually exclusive for non-aborted, non-auto-rewarded trials. The fallback `'other'` handles any edge cases, though in practice all valid trials should match one of the four outcomes.\n   357→\n   358→## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Trial outcome*?\n   359→\n   360→i. Trial outcomes are mapped to integer codes (0–3) via a fixed mapping. The integer code is constant across all time bins within a trial.\n   361→\n   362→ii.\n   363→```python\n   364→outcome_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(TRIAL_OUTCOMES)}\n   365→...\n   366→outcome_code = outcome_to_code.get(t['trial_outcome'], -1)\n   367→outcome_row = np.full(n_frames, outcome_code, dtype=np.int8)\n   368→```\n   369→\n   370→iii. The mapping order matches `TRIAL_OUTCOMES = ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']`. The mapping is stored in `metadata['outcome_to_code']` for recovery.\n   371→\n   372→## 7-c. How is `output` *Trial outcome* aligned with the neural data?\n   373→\n   374→N/A — trial outcome is a per-trial constant, not a time-varying signal.\n   375→\n   376→## 8. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n   377→\n   378→i. Several cases are handled:\n   379→- **Failed sessions**: If `extract_session_data` or `segment_trials` throws an exception, the session is skipped with a warning.\n   380→- **Truncated trials**: If `stop_time` extends past the recording, the trial is clipped to the end. Trials with no frames (`end_idx <= start_idx`) are skipped.\n   381→- **Missing behavioral data**: NaN values from interpolation (running speed or pupil diameter outside the recorded range) are mapped to bin 0 during discretization.\n   382→- **Few trials**: Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded.\n   383→\n   384→ii.\n   385→```python\n   386→try:\n   387→    session_data = extract_session_data(bc, sess_exps)\n   388→    trials = segment_trials(session_data)\n   389→except Exception as e:\n   390→    print(f'FAILED: {e}')\n   391→    continue\n   392→...\n   393→if end_idx > T:\n   394→    end_idx = T\n   395→if end_idx <= start_idx:\n   396→    continue\n   397→...\n   398→out[np.isnan(values)] = 0\n   399→...\n   400→if len(trials) < 2:\n   401→    continue\n   402→```\n   403→\n   404→iii. The try/except ensures a single bad session doesn't crash the entire pipeline. NaN-to-0 mapping is a conservative default that avoids propagating missing data into the discretized output.\n   405→\n   406→## 9-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n   407→\n   408→i. The most time-consuming step is loading each experiment via `bc.get_behavior_ophys_experiment()`, which downloads/reads large neural and behavioral data arrays from the S3 cache. This is I/O bound.\n   409→\n   410→ii. N/A\n   411→\n   412→iii. Each experiment contains full-session dF/F traces for all neurons, plus running speed, eye tracking, and trials data. The SDK caches files locally after first download, but reading them is still the bottleneck.\n   413→\n   414→## 9-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n   415→\n   416→i. The per-trial loop in `segment_trials` iterates over each valid trial sequentially. The `np.searchsorted` and array slicing could theoretically be vectorized across all trials, but the loop is not a bottleneck compared to data loading.\n   417→\n   418→ii. N/A\n   419→\n   420→iii. The per-trial loop is simple and readable. Data loading dominates runtime, so vectorizing the trial loop would yield negligible speedup.\n   421→\n   422→## 9-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n   423→\n   424→No processing is repeated. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. --- Neural: merge dF/F across planes ---\n    dff_list = []\n    plane_labels = []\n    for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n        dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n        area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n        depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n        plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\n    neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # Temporal alignment is based on ophys timestamps, and trials are segmented using the AllenSDK `trials` table.\n     8→\n     9→## Reference-Matched Decisions\n    10→\n    11→### Session unit\n    12→\n    13→Each decoder \"session\" is one Allen `BehaviorOphysExperiment` NWB file, not one unique behavior session. This matches the AllenSDK data model and avoids incorrectly merging different imaging planes from multiscope sessions into one neuron matrix. As a result:\n    14→\n    15→- 199 converted decoder sessions correspond to 171 unique `ophys_session_id` values.\n    16→- The local active cache contains 202 experiment files across 174 unique behavior sessions.\n    17→\n    18→### Local data subset\n    19→\n    20→Only NWB files physically present under:\n    21→\n    22→`/app/data/visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0/behavior_ophys_experiments`\n    23→\n    24→were used. The converter keeps only experiments with `passive == False`, which locally yields:\n    36→\n    37→- `OPHYS_1_images_A`: 54 converted sessions\n    38→- `OPHYS_3_images_A`: 55 converted sessions\n    39→- `OPHYS_4_images_B`: 45 converted sessions\n    40→- `OPHYS_6_images_B`: 45 converted sessions\n    41→\n    42→Project-code coverage after conversion:\n    43→\n    44→- `VisualBehavior`: 165 sessions\n    45→- `VisualBehaviorMultiscope`: 34 sessions\n    46→\n    47→Experience-level coverage after conversion:\n    48→\n    49→- `Familiar`: 109 sessions\n    50→- `Novel 1`: 37 sessions\n    51→- `Novel >1`: 53 sessions\n    52→\n    53→### Trial inclusion\n    54→\n    55→Per instructions and Allen trial definitions, trials are kept only when:\n    56→\n    57→- `go == True` or `catch == True`\n    58→- `aborted == False`\n    59→- `auto_rewarded == False`\n    60→\n    61→This retains the experimentally meaningful go/catch trials while excluding premature-lick resets and free-reward trials.\n    62→\n    63→### Neural signal\n    64→\n    65→Neural activity uses AllenSDK discrete calcium events from `dataset.events[\"events\"]`.\n    66→\n    67→This matches the paper's statement that neural analyses were performed on detected calcium events rather than raw fluorescence. The converter intentionally does not use dF/F traces and does not use `filtered_events`.\n    68→\n    69→AllenSDK default ROI validity filtering is left intact, so invalid ROIs are excluded automatically when loading each `BehaviorOphysExperiment`.\n    70→\n    71→### Temporal alignment and binning\n    72→\n    73→Trials are segmented from `trial.start_time` to `trial.stop_time`. Temporal alignment is based on ophys timestamps, and trials are segmented using the AllenSDK `trials` table.\n\n## Reference-Matched Decisions\n\n### Session unit\n\nEach decoder \"session\" is one Allen `BehaviorOphysExperiment` NWB file, not one unique behavior session. This matches the AllenSDK data model and avoids incorrectly merging different imaging planes from multiscope sessions into one neuron matrix. As a result:\n\n- 199 converted decoder sessions correspond to 171 unique `ophys_session_id` values.\n- The local active cache contains 202 experiment files across 174 unique behavior sessions.\n\n### Local data subset\n\nOnly NWB files physically present under:\n\n`/app/data/visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0/behavior_ophys_experiments`\n\nwere used. The local cache contains:\n\n- 284 total experiment files\n- 202 active experiment files\n- 174 unique active behavior/ophys sessions\n- 38 mice\n\nThis is smaller than the full dataset described in the paper, so paper-level totals such as 376 imaging sessions / 82 mice cannot be matched exactly from the local copy.\n\n### Active-task filtering\n\nThe whitepaper and paper distinguish active change-detection sessions from passive viewing. The converter keeps only experiments with `passive == False`, which locally yields:\n\n- `OPHYS_1_images_A`: 54 converted sessions\n- `OPHYS_3_images_A`: 55 converted sessions\n- `OPHYS_4_images_B`: 45 converted sessions\n- `OPHYS_6_images_B`: 45 converted sessions\n\nProject-code coverage after conversion:\n\n- `VisualBehavior`: 165 sessions\n- `VisualBehaviorMultiscope`: 34 sessions\n\nExperience-level coverage after conversion:\n\n- `Familiar`: 109 sessions\n- `Novel 1`: 37 sessions\n- `Novel >1`: 53 sessions\n\n### Trial inclusion\n\nPer instructions and Allen trial definitions, trials are kept only when:\n\n- `go == True` or `catch == True`\n- `aborted == False`\n- `auto_rewarded == False`\n\nThis retains the experimentally meaningful go/catch trials while excluding premature-lick resets and free-reward trials.\n\n### Neural signal\n\nNeural activity uses AllenSDK discrete calcium events from `dataset.events[\"events\"]`.\n\nThis matches the paper's statement that neural analyses were performed on detected calcium events rather than raw fluorescence. The converter intentionally does not use dF/F traces and does not use `filtered_events`.\n\nAllenSDK default ROI validity filtering is left intact, so invalid ROIs are excluded automatically when loading each `BehaviorOphysExperiment`.\n\n### Temporal alignment and binning\n\nTrials are segmented from `trial.start_time` to `trial.stop_time`. Within each trial:\n\n- a common 100 ms bin size is used for every session\n- bin timestamps are bin centers\n- neural samples are aligned by nearest ophys timestamp\n- running speed is linearly interpolated to those timestamps\n- pupil diameter is linearly interpolated to those timestamps\n\nThe local dataset mixes single-plane and multiscope experiments. Their native frame intervals differ:\n\n- median native frame interval: 32.32 ms\n- min: 32.31 ms\n- max: 93.23 ms\n\nUsing 100 ms bins preserves compatibility across both acquisition modes while staying close to the slower multiscope sampling interval.\n\n### Stimulus labels\n\nStimulus labels come only from `stimulus_presentations` rows whose `stimulus_block_name` contains `change_detection`.\n\nFor each trial:\n\n- `image_identity` is the image shown during flashed image epochs\n- all other times are labeled `gray`\n- omissions remain `gray`\n- `image_change` is `1` only during flashed epochs with `is_change == True`, else `0`\n\nThis matches the task structure described in the references: 250 ms image flash followed by 500 ms gray, with 5% omissions and no omissions on the change image or the immediately preceding image.\n\n### Behavioral outputs\n\nThe decoder outputs are:\n\n1. `image_identity`\n2. `image_change`\n3. `running_speed_bin`\n4. `pupil_diameter_bin`\n5. `trial_outcome`\n\n`trial_outcome` is static within each trial and encoded as:\n\n- `0`: `hit`\n- `1`: `miss`\n- `2`: `false_alarm`\n- `3`: `correct_reject`\n\nRunning and pupil are discretized into global quintiles over all kept trial time bins in the converted dataset.\n\nPupil diameter is computed as equivalent diameter from AllenSDK `pupil_area`:\n\n`diameter = 2 * sqrt(area / pi)`\n\n## Exclusions\n\nNo sessions were dropped for missing events or too few valid trials. Three experiment files were excluded because pupil data were entirely invalid:\n\n- `795953296`\n- `806456687`\n- `833631914`\n\nFinal converted dataset:\n\n- 199 experiment-level sessions\n- 171 unique behavior/ophys sessions\n- 38 subjects\n- 51,075 trials\n- 29,168 neurons\n\nBrain-region composition of the local subset is heavily V1-skewed:\n\n- `VISp`: 29,006 neurons\n- `VISl`: 162 neurons\n\n## Sanity Checks\n\n### Structural checks\n\n`train_decoder.py --verify-only` passes on both the full and sample datasets.\n\n- `/app/verification_full_out.txt`\n- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`\n\nThe decoder-format structure is valid, with:\n\n- empty decoder inputs represented as `(0, T)` arrays\n- 5 categorical outputs\n- at least 2 trials per kept session\n\n### Distribution checks\n\nFull-dataset output fractions:\n\n- `image_identity`: `gray` = 0.668\n- `image_change`: `change` = 0.027\n- `running_speed_bin`: each bin = 0.200\n- `pupil_diameter_bin`: each bin = 0.200\n- `trial_outcome`: `hit` 0.303, `miss` 0.571, `false_alarm` 0.017, `correct_reject` 0.108\n\nImportant consistency observations:\n\n- The `gray` fraction is close to the expected 2/3 from the 250 ms image + 500 ms gray task cadence.\n- `image_change` is rare, as expected for the roving-baseline change-detection design.\n- Running and pupil bins are exactly balanced because they are defined by global percentiles.\n\n### Sample decoder training\n\nThe sample dataset is a fixed representative subset spanning both project codes, both local brain regions, and all four active session types. It contains 11 sessions, 1,986 trials, and 1,133 neurons.\n\nSample experiment IDs:\n\n- `775614751`\n- `792813858`\n- `794381992`\n- `795073741`\n- `795076128`\n- `788490510`\n- `796105304`\n- `951980475`\n- `958527485`\n- `957759566`\n- `960410038`\n\nSample training results from `/app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt`:\n\n- validation `image_identity`: 0.1705 vs 0.0588 chance\n- validation `image_change`: 0.5947 vs 0.5000 chance\n- validation `running_speed_bin`: 0.2258 vs 0.2000 chance\n- validation `pupil_diameter_bin`: 0.2352 vs 0.2000 chance\n- validation `trial_outcome`: 0.2468 vs 0.2500 chance\n\nThis indicates that stimulus identity, stimulus change, running, and pupil carry usable signal in the converted neural data. Trial outcome is near chance on the small representative subset.\n\n### Full decoder training\n\nFull training on `/app/converted_data.pkl` completed successfully. Final validation balanced accuracy from `/app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`:\n\n- validation `image_identity`: 0.1488 vs 0.0588 chance\n- validation `image_change`: 0.5694 vs 0.5000 chance\n- validation `running_speed_bin`: 0.2295 vs 0.2000 chance\n- validation `pupil_diameter_bin`: 0.2467 vs 0.2000 chance\n- validation `trial_outcome`: 0.2582 vs 0.2500 chance\n\nThe full dataset therefore preserves decodable information for the main time-varying stimulus and behavior variables, with trial-outcome decoding only slightly above chance.\n\n## Warnings and Caveats\n\n`train_decoder.py` reports warnings for some trials whose neural event matrices are entirely zero. These are not format errors. They arise when valid event traces contain no detected events during a given trial window, which is plausible for sparse event representations, especially in low-neuron multiscope planes.\n\nThe converted dataset is intentionally experiment-plane based, not cross-plane merged. Same alignment approach as all other time-varying outputs.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n\ni. The `running_speed` attribute is the SDK's standard interface for locomotion data.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Running speed is linearly interpolated to the 100 ms time bin centers. NaN/non-finite values in the source are excluded before interpolation. Values are then discretized into 5 equal percentile bins (quintiles) computed globally across all sessions. Bin edges are at the 20th, 40th, 60th, and 80th percentiles.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interp_signal(source_times, source_values, target_times):\n    valid = np.isfinite(source_times) & np.isfinite(source_values)\n    ...\n    interp = np.interp(target_times, times, values, left=values[0], right=values[-1])\n    return interp.astype(np.float32)\n\nrunning_edges = np.percentile(running_values, [20, 40, 60, 80]).astype(np.float64)\n\ndef remap_to_bins(values, edges):\n    return np.digitize(values, edges, right=False).astype(np.int16)\n```\n\niii. Global percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts. The AI uses `np.interp` with edge clamping (left/right fill) rather than `scipy.interpolate.interp1d` with NaN fill.\n\n## 5-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Running speed is discretized into 5 bins using 4 edges at the 20th, 40th, 60th, and 80th percentiles computed globally. `np.digitize` maps values to bins 0-4.\n\nii.\n```python\nrunning_edges = np.percentile(running_values, [20, 40, 60, 80]).astype(np.float64)\nrunning_bins = remap_to_bins(trial[\"running\"], running_edges)\n```\n\niii. This produces 5 quintile bins with approximately equal numbers of samples.\n\n## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Running speed is interpolated directly to the same 100 ms `target_times` array used for neural data, ensuring alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\nrunning_trial = interp_signal(\n    running_df[\"timestamps\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    running_df[\"speed\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    target_times,\n)\n```\n\niii. Same time bin centers for all data streams.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Pupil diameter* derived from?\n\ni. Pupil diameter is derived from `dataset.eye_tracking`, using the `pupil_area` column. The diameter is computed as `2 * sqrt(pupil_area / pi)`.\n\nii.\n```python\neye_df = dataset.eye_tracking\npupil_diameter = 2.0 * np.sqrt(\n    np.asarray(eye_df[\"pupil_area\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64)) / np.pi\n)\n```\n\niii. The AI computes equivalent circular diameter from pupil area, rather than using `pupil_width` directly.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n\ni. Pupil area is converted to equivalent diameter, then linearly interpolated to the 100 ms time bin centers. NaN/non-finite values are excluded during interpolation. The result is discretized into 5 quintile bins using global percentile edges. No explicit blink removal is performed before interpolation.\n\nii.\n```python\npupil_diameter = 2.0 * np.sqrt(\n    np.asarray(eye_df[\"pupil_area\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64)) / np.pi\n)\npupil_trial = interp_signal(\n    eye_df[\"timestamps\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    pupil_diameter,\n    target_times,\n)\n...\npupil_edges = np.percentile(pupil_values, [20, 40, 60, 80]).astype(np.float64)\npupil_bins = remap_to_bins(trial[\"pupil\"], pupil_edges)\n```\n\niii. The AI's `interp_signal` function filters out non-finite values before interpolation, which partially handles blink artifacts (since `pupil_area` may be NaN during blinks). However, there is no explicit use of the `likely_blink` flag.\n\n## 6-c. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Same approach as running speed: 5 quintile bins using global percentile edges at the 20th, 40th, 60th, and 80th percentiles.\n\nii.\n```python\npupil_edges = np.percentile(pupil_values, [20, 40, 60, 80]).astype(np.float64)\npupil_bins = remap_to_bins(trial[\"pupil\"], pupil_edges)\n```\n\niii. Same global percentile approach as running speed.\n\n## 6-d. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same as running speed — interpolated to the same 100 ms `target_times` array.\n\nii.\n```python\npupil_trial = interp_signal(\n    eye_df[\"timestamps\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    pupil_diameter,\n    target_times,\n)\n```\n\niii. Same alignment approach as all other time-varying outputs.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Trial outcome is derived from the boolean columns `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, and `correct_reject` in the trials table.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef outcome_to_index(trial_row: pd.Series) -> int | None:\n    if bool(trial_row[\"hit\"]):\n        return 0\n    if bool(trial_row[\"miss\"]):\n        return 1\n    if bool(trial_row[\"false_alarm\"]):\n        return 2\n    if bool(trial_row[\"correct_reject\"]):\n        return 3\n    return None\n```\n\niii. These four columns are the SDK's canonical trial outcome labels for the change detection task. Trials where none of these are True are excluded (returns None).\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Trial outcome*?\n\ni. Trial outcomes are mapped to integer codes (0=hit, 1=miss, 2=false_alarm, 3=correct_reject). The code is constant across all time bins within a trial. Trials with no matching outcome are skipped entirely.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_idx = outcome_to_index(trial_row)\nif outcome_idx is None:\n    continue\n...\noutcome = np.full(t, outcome_idx, dtype=np.int16)\n```\n\niii. The mapping is consistent with the reference. Trials without a valid outcome are excluded rather than assigned a fallback.\n\n## 8. The running and pupil raw data arrays are re-extracted from the DataFrame on every trial iteration rather than being precomputed once per session.\n\nii.\n```python\n# First call: availability check\nrunning_interp_source = interp_signal(\n    running_df[\"timestamps\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    running_df[\"speed\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    np.array([ophys_timestamps[0]], dtype=np.float64),\n)\n\n# Then per trial:\nrunning_trial = interp_signal(\n    running_df[\"timestamps\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    running_df[\"speed\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64),\n    target_times,\n)\n```\n\niii. The `.to_numpy()` calls inside the trial loop recreate numpy arrays from the DataFrame on each iteration.\n\n## 9-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. The AI computes pupil diameter as `2*sqrt(area/pi)` from `pupil_area`, but this monotonic transformation is unnecessary since the result is immediately discretized into percentile bins (the bin assignments would be identical if using `pupil_area` directly, since percentile ranks are preserved under monotonic transformations).\n\nii.\n```python\npupil_diameter = 2.0 * np.sqrt(\n    np.asarray(eye_df[\"pupil_area\"].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64)) / np.pi\n)\n```\n\niii. Percentile-based binning is invariant to monotonic transformations, so the area-to-diameter conversion is mathematically redundant for the downstream discretization step.\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}
